No. 79. ON THREE PILLARS OF THE SOUTH ENCLOSURE.!

chaturvédimangalam in Nallair-nadu, a district of Nittavinéda-valanaddu, 
set up a copper image of Durgd4-ParaméSvari and presented a number of ornaments to it.

No. 82. Own THE BANE OF THE SOUTH ENCLOSURE.” 


This inscription is dated in the 7th year of Rajéndra-Choéla I. The king’s 
conquests end with the subjugation of the island of Sindimattivu. As we know that the 
invasion of the ‘‘ many ancient islands” immediately preceded this event and that it took 
place in the 6th year,* it may be concluded that the conquest of Sandimattivu must have 
taken place in the 7th year of the king’s reign = A.D. 1018-19. The Tiruvalangddu 
plates' tell us that Ra jéndra-C hé la advanced against the Western Country because he had 
heard of the disgrace which the kings of the earth had suffered at the hands of ParaSurama. 
Not finding him on earth the Ch 6] a king desired to conquer the country reclaimed by him, 
which was protected against foreign invasion by the greatness of ParaSurama’s penance, It 
is cvident that the conquest of the island of Sandimattivu was the most important 
event of the campaign against K érala which must have taken place in A.D. 1018-19. 

The subjoined inscription registers an endowment in money in favour of the image 
of Srikanthamtrtigal set up by Prithivimahadéviyar, queen of Rajaraja- 
déva. The money was lent out on interest to the members of the assembly of Nara- 
ginga-chaturvédimangalam, a brahmadéya in Avir-ktrram, a subdivision of 
Nittavinédda-valanddu. The interest was to be paid in kind and amounted to 
thirty-seven ‘alam, one tint and one padakku of paddy. 

This and the next record belong to a class of Tanjore inscriptions which are dated 
duving the reign of RAjéndra-Chéla I. and register endowments by groups of men. To 
cach of these groups a shrine in the temple was “ attached ” by order of the king.’

Sri-R4éjéndra-Chéladéva, who,--in (/7s) life of high prosperity *, while fortune, having 
become constant, was increasing, (and) while the goddess of the great earth, the goddess of 
victory in battle, and the matchless goddess of fame rejoiced in having become his great 
queens,—conquered, with (/zs) great warlike army, [daiturai-nadu; Vanavasi, whose un- 
broken hedge of forest (drees) was extending ; Kollippakkai, whose walls were surrounded 
by ‘ $ulli? (trees) ; the camp of Mannai, whose strength (ze. fortifications) was unapproach- 
able ; the crown of the king of fram, who came to close quarters in fighting ; the exceedingly 
beautiful crown of the queen of that (Ang) ; the beautiful crown and Indra’s pearl-necklace 
which the king of the South (7.e. the Pandya) had previously deposited with that (king 
of fra m); the whole fra-mandalam on the transparent sea ; the crown praised by many 
and the garland (emz¢ting) beautiful rays,—family treasures, which the (dings of) Kérala, 
whose armies overcame (opponents), rightfully wore; many ancient islands, whose old and 
great guard was the sea, which resounds with conches ; the crown of pure gold, worthy of 
Lakshmi, which Paraguraima, having thought of the fortifications of the impregnable 
Sandimattiv u, had deposited (here) when, enraged in battle, (he) bound the kings twenty- 
one times,—there was engraved on stone (the name of} the village which had received on 
interest from Chandésvaradéva,—the first servant of the supreme ford who has been 
pleased to take up gladly his abode in (the temple called) Sri-Rajaraj é$vara,—the money 
which had been deposited until the seventh year {of the reign) of the lord Sri- Raj éndra- 
Chéladéva on account of the offerings and other expenses required for (the image of) 
Srikanthamfrtigal, which Prithivimahadéviyar, the consort of the lord Sri- 
Rajarajadéva, had set up in the temple of Sri-RAjarajésvaram udaiydr and which

No. 85. On A PILLAR OF THE WEST ENCLOSURE, ° 


This inscription belongs to the reign of RAjéndra-Chéla I. and records the gift of 
copper, zinc and bell-metal vessels to the image.of Gana patiy4r set up in the principal temple. 
This image was probably in the central shrine and was known as Alaiyattu-Pillaiyar! 
perhaps to distinguish him from the Parivaralaiyattu-Pillaiydr® set up apparently in 
the enclosing verandah of tire temple. A portion of the inscription is damaged (11. 23 to 36).

No. 86. On a PILLAR OF THE WEST ENCLOSURE." 

On this pillar are recorded gifts to the shrine of Ganapatiydar in the porivdrdlaya 
made (1) by king RajarAja until the twenty-ninth year of his reign, (2) by Adittan 
Stryan alias Tennavan-Mfivéndavélin, the mauager of the Rajardjésvara temple 
and (3) by the same donor until the 8rd year of Rajéndra-Chélal. The date of No. (2) 
is not mentioned. This inscription shows that the 29th year of Rajaraja I. could not have 
been very far removed from the 3rd year of RAjéndra-Chéla 1.

recorded) on stone after (they) had been weighed by the stone called Dakshinaméru- 
Vitankan and after the gold had been weighed by the stone called Adaval lan.

No. 90. In THE SECOND GOPURA, LEFT OF ENTRANCE.' 

This inscription records the gift, by the priest {éanagiva Pandita, of 8 gilt copper- 
pots “ until the 29th year of RA jardjadéva’’ and of one receptacle for sacred ashes, in the 
2nd year of RAjéndra-Chéla. A ninth pot was presented by Pavana-Pidaran, the 
Saiva dchdrya of the temple, in the 3rd year of Rajéndra-Chéladéva. Sarvaégiva 
Paudita mentioned in No. 20, dated during the 19th year of the same king ’ was apparently 
a successor of Pavana-Pidaran in the office of Saiva dchdrya of the temple. 

The gilt copper-pots were all intended to be used as pinnacles. Nine of them were 
presented, of which one is said to have been for “ the temple of the lord” (paragraph 9) 
and another for the temple of the lord Sri-Rajarajé$varamudaiyar" (paragraph 11). The 
other seven appear to have been intended as pinnacles for the shrines of the regents of the 
eight quarters. The regents are erght in number, vz. Indra, Agni, Yama, Nirriti, 
Varuna, Vayu, Sémaand Igana. As the inscription is damaged, we have not got the 
names of all of them. The names of Nairitiyar, Agnidévar, Yamaraja and Varuna- 
rijaare preserved. The shrine of Indra who is the regent of the Last seems to have been 
in the second gépura for which five pinnacles had apparently been provided by the king

No. 91. ON THE NORTH WALL OF THE CENTRAL SHRINE, LOWRR 1IER.* 

This inscription contains a list of silver utensils presented by king Rijardjadéva to 
the temple. They are said to have borne the names Sivapidaéégaran and Sri-Raja- 
rijan* and to have been procured from three sources, viz. (1) the king’s treasures, (2) the 
booty acquired in the war against the Chéra king and the Pandyas in Malai-nédu and 
(3) the silver seized in the same campaign. The list, however, begins with the gifts made 
trom the treasures seized after defeating the Ch éra kingand the Pandyasin Malai-nadu.

1 Compare above, p. 215, text-line 8. 2 Hudtiiat-kal occurs also in No. 6, above, text-line 1.

£16 INSCRIPTIONS OF THE RATARAJESVARA. TEMPLE, -TANJAVUR. 


No mention of the other two items occurs in the preserved portion of the inscription. But as 
lines 2 to 4 are considerably damaged; causing almost the complete disappearance of several 
paragraphs in each case, we cannot be quite sure that the other two items are not mentioned. 
In fact, as the enumeration of the vessels is repeated in different parts of the inscription, it 
may be presumed that the other items are also referred to in the damaged portions. For 
instance, bowls (manda) are enumerated in paragraphs 48 to 59 and in paragraphs 128-9 ; 
censers (kalaSappinat) in paragraphs 65 to 75 as well as in paragraph 122; small baskets 
in paragraph $1 as well as in 130; spittoons (padikkam) in paragraph 107 as well 
as in 139. It is very unlikely that the utensils described in the latter part of the inscription 
were omitted by mistake when the earlier part was engraved. At the end of the inscription 
three images of Sri-Vasudéva made of silver are mentioned. Perhaps these were made 
from the silver seized in the campaign against the Chéra king and the Pandyas in Malai- 
nidu. The gold emblems, corals and ornaments seized in the same war are referred to in 
No. 1, paragraph 51, No. 3, paragraphs 5 and 6, No. 59, paragraphs 2, 3, 4, 9 and I! and in 
No. 93 below. ° 

It is worthy of note that this is the only inscription of the.temple which mentions 
presents made of silver. Most of the other inscriptions record gifts of gold. 1+ looks as if 
the king had more gold and precious stones at his disposal than silver.

Hail! Prosperity! The sacred silver utensils (rupparigalam), etc., bearing the sacred 
names Sivapddaségaran and Sri-Rajardjan presented by the lord Sri-Rajaraja- 
déva until the twenty-ninth year (of lis reign) to the supreme lord of the Sri-Rajara- 
jéSvara (lemple),—(viz.) (1) the sacred silver utensils, ete., presented from his (own) 
treasures (2) the sacred silver utensils, ete.,* seized after defeating the Chéra king and 
the Pandyas in Malai-naidu and (3) the sacred utensils, ete., made from the silver acquired 
(as bvoty) after defeating the same (enemies) —[ were engraved (/.e. recorded) on stone after 
(they) had been weighed with the stone] called Adavallan.

No. 92. ON THE SOUTH WALL, THIRD ‘TIER. * 


This inscription records the assignment to the Tanjore temple of certain villages in 
Tondai-naidu or Jayangonda-Chéla- mandalam, Pandi-nadu or Rajaraja- 
mandalam, Ganga-padi, Nulamba-pédi also called Nigarili-Sdra-padi, Malai- 
nédu and ene (Ceylon) or Mummadi-Chéla-mandalam. This is, consequently, the 
euptinuation of No. 4, which registers the gift by Rajardjadéva of villages in the 
Chéla country (Séra-mandalam) and in other countries (puramandulanyal). The villages 
of the former are enumerated in Nos. 4 and 5, and those of the other provinces in the present 
inscription. The latter is considerably damaged. The number of villages outside the 
Ch6la country whose revenues were assigned to the temple is, however, comparatively small. 
None the less, they show that the conquest of those provinces claimed for Rajarajadéva in 
his numerous Tamil inscriptions was not a mere victorious inroad but a more or less perma- 
nent occupation of them. The omission of minute details of measurement which are 
mentioned in connection with the villages of the Chéla country is a point worthy of note. 
The previous owners and ryots are declared, in several cases, to have been replaced. 
Evidently, the original holdings were altered and those holders who were prepared to accept 
the conqueror’s proposals were pufin. Outside the Ch6la country, the revenue payable 
to the king was partly in kind and partly in gold or money, while the villages paying 
revenue in money was comparatively rare in the Chola country. In Ceylon, where the 
conditions were apparently different, the villages were required to remit the revenue in 
paddy, money and iluppar-pdl,* which was evidently required for the lamps to be burnt in 
the temple. This again shows the king’s solicitude to'provide for the requirements of the 
temple. as 


' See note 2 on page 419 above. 2 No, 37 of 1897- 

* The word iuppai-p4él means ‘ milk of (the tree) Bassia longifolia.’ It was apparently ha oil expressed 
from the seeds of the tree that had to be supplied to the rane temple. This oil is even now used largely 
for burning lamps in the Tamil country. 


No. 92. ON THE SOUTH WALL, THIRD TIER. 425 


Among the villages ‘and subdivisions of provinces mentioned in the inscription, 
Oy m4-n4du ‘is‘an ancient territerid] designation known to early Tamil literature. It is the 
tract of country, in which the modern town of Tindivanam in the South Arcot District is 
situated! Consequently, the-village of PérAydr in Oym4-nddu has to be looked for in 
the neighbourhood of Tindivanam. Padi-nidu in Ganga-padi bas been located by 
Mr. Rice in the Mysore District of the Mysore State.2 The village of Altir in Padi- 
nadu may be identical with Altir in the Chamardjnagar taluka of the Mysore District 
where a Tamil inscription of the time of the Chéla king Vijaya-Rajéndradéva has 
been found by Mr. Rice (Ch. 69). The villages of Kuéavdrand Kfidaltr belonged to 
Parivai-nadu in Nulamba-paddi alas Nigarili-Séra-padi. Parivai-nadu 
probably owes its name to the Bana capital Parivaior Parivipuri,*’ which may be 
identified with Parigi in the Anantapur District. Nulamba-p4di also called Nigarili- 
Séra-padi appears to have extended into the Anantapur District, as Hémivati (called 
Pefijeru in inscriptions) was its ancient cupital.' I have not been able to identify 
Pudukkédu in Vellappa-nidu, a district of Malai-nédu. Four villages of 
Malai-nadu seem to have been assigned (paragraphs 8 to 11). But their assessment is not 
specified. 

Two territorial divisions of Ceylon are mentioned, v/z., Mappigumbu-kéttiydram 
and Kanakkan-kéttiydram. The former was called Rajaraja-valanaddu and the 
latter Vikrama-Chéla- valanddu, while Ceylon itself was known as Mummadi- 
Chéla-mandalam. The fiscal terms pévumanaitium pdyappdgddi and tardnduvachchél I 
am unable to explain. But the fact that five villages in Ceylon were required to contribute 
to the expenses of the Tanjore temple shows that the island was a province of the Chéla 
empire during the reign of Rajaraja I.

No. 98. ON THE SoUTH WALL, THIRD TIER. 

This inscription enumerates the jewelled ornaments presented by the king out of the 
treasures seized from the Chéras and Pand:yas and outof his owntreasures. They 
comprise necklaces; armlets ; bracelets ; rings ; sandals made of wood, covered with gold plates 
and set with jewels; and others, Four of the rings had the nine gems set on them, viz. 
diamond, sapphire, pearl, topaz, cinnamon stone, coral, emerald, lapiz lazuli and ruby. The 
mention of the nine gems occurs only here among the Tanjore inscriptions. Another interest- 
ing ornament is what : known as Sénagachchidukkin-kidu. It isnot clear what a sidubku was 
and where it was worn.’ But that the ornament was made after the fashion of the Jénakas 
is very interesting. The donor in No. 28 above was apparently a Sénagan. One of the 
donors mentioned in No. 95 below (paragraphs 45, 74 and 75) is the Jonaka Savar 
Parafijédi. TheJ 6nakas were, therefore, ianwelt in T'anjore about the time of which 
we are speaking. The Muhammadans who are at present denoted by the term jénaka are 
altogether precluded, as they had not come to the south at the time. Perhaps, there were 
some reminiscences of Greek influence which had prevailed at the time when mercantile 
relations existed between the South of India and the Roman Empire. It may also be that 
Sav tir Paratij6di was an Arab from the west coast. In any case, it is worthy of note 
that he had assumed the purely Hindu name P arafijédi. 

This inseription is probably a continuation of Nos. 3 and 59 above, which give a a 
of ornaments made.of gold and jewels presented by the king.

Hail ! Prosperity: In the 29th year (of the reiyn) of king Rajakésarivarman alias 
Sri- Rajaraja déva, who, —in the belief that, as well as the goddess of fortune, the goddess 
of the great earth had become his wife,—was pleased to destroy the ships (ad) Kandaltir- 
Salai, and conquered by (fs) exceedingly brave and victorious army, Véngai-nadu, 
Ganga-pidi, Tadigai-pidi, Nulamba-pidi, Kudamalai-nédu, Kollam, 
Kalingam, fra-mandalam, (the conguest of which) made (him) famous (in) the ais 
directions, and the seven and a half lakshas of Iratta- p&di,—and who deprived the 
Seriyas of (their) splendour at the very time when his strength shone so as to be 
worshipped in ail quarters at all times—the sacred ornaments (tiruvdbharanangal) (set) 
with jewels (ra‘nam), presented until the 29th year (of the king’s reign) to the supreme lord’ , 
(paramasvémin) of the Sri-Rajarajésvara (temple) by the lord Sri-Rajarajadéva, from 
the treasures which he had seized after defeating the Chéra king and the Pandyas, and

No. 94. ON ‘rHE OUTSIDE OF THE NORTH ENCLOSURE. 

This is not a fresh inscription but only a continuation of No. 63, where the first two 
lines of it are published.! For convenience of reference a separate number has been 
given to this portion of the inscription. It contains a list of shepherds who had to supply 
chee for lamps to the Rajarajésvara temple. To each shepherd were assigned either 
48 cows or 96 ewes. In a few cases, the equivalent of the latter was given, viz. 8 she- 
buffaloes and 48 ewes (paragraphs 70, 71 and 73), 2 she-buffaloes and 84 ewes (paragraph 
72) or 4 she-buffaloes and 72 ewes (paragraph 74). Evidently each she-buffalo was 
considered, as far as its milk-yielding capacity was concerned, equivalent to 6 ewes.” The 
names of each shepherd’s dependents * who were held responsible for the supply of ghee 
are also mentioned. : 

Naturally, the shepherds residing in the neighbourhood of Tanjore received a large number 
of she-buffaloes, cows and ewes. They belonged to ten streets described as being outside 
(purambadi) Tanjore, vz. Gandharva-teru (ée. the street of the musicians), Vi lligal- 
teru (ze. the street of the bowmen), Anaikkaduvar-teru (ie. the street of those 
who cook for elephants), Anaiyatkal-ternu (i.e. the street of the elephant troops), 
Panmaiyar-teru', Madaippalli-teru (ze. the street adjoining the temple kitchen), 


ea aa NS ten ie Deca eee ee at See, ee OO oh in osha Se eee 

' Nos. 9fand 95 were copied in 1897. They became completely accessible only after excavations had 
been made below the ground level clese tothe onter side of the north wall of the enclosure of the temple. 
The excavations were filled in soon after the inscriptions had been copied. 

* This appears also from line 1 of this inscription published on page 249 above. 

* The number of persons jointly responsible is generally 5. In asingle instance (paragraph 77) the 
number isreduced tov, Ina Mélpadi inscription (Vol. III, p. 29) the responsibility of those who stood 
security in a similar cuse is set forth as follows :—‘* Tf he dies, absconds, or gets into prison, fetters (or) chains, 
we, all these aforesaid persons, are bound to supply ghee for burning the holy lamp as long as the moon and 


No. 94. ON THE OUTSIDE OF THE NORTH ENCLOSURE. 437 


Virasérapperunderu, Rajavidyddbarapperunderu, Jayangondasérapperun- 
deru and Strasikhamanipperunderu, and to a eleventh strect described as being within 
(ulldlai) Tanjore,wz. Sdliyatteru (ze. the weavers’ street). The last four of the streets 
outside the town were evidently thoroughfares named after one or more members of the 
Chéla family,! while the others appear to have been occupied either wholly or in part by 
people engaged in particular professions. Some of the shepherds belonged to three bazars 
(angadi) said to be outside the town, viz, Tribhuvanamadévippérangadi-, 
Kongavalar-angadi' and Rajaraéja-Brahma-mahardjan-angadi*; and the rest to 
the following quarters also described as being outside the town :—Abhim aénabhitishana- 
terinda-vélam, Uyyakkondan-terinda-tirumaijanattar-vélam,® Uttamasi- 
liyar-vélam, Radjardja-terinda-Pandi-tirumafijanattar-vélam * and Arumo- 
ridéva-terinda-tirupparigalattir®- vélam. Among these five long names of 
localities, Abhimanabhfshana’?, Uyyakkondin, Uttamagili‘, Rajarfja and 
Arumoridéva wereall apparently the names or surnames of the king himself. At any rate, 
we know for certain that Rajardja and Arumoridéva were borne by him.” 

In the case of the shepherds who did not belong to the city of Tanjore, the name of the 
village where each of them was living and the district in which it was situated are mentioned. 
Thus we get valuable information about the ancient geography of the Chéla country. 
The following are the names of distiicts mentioned in this connection :—Arumoridéva- 
valanidu, Padndyakuligani-valanidu, Nittavinédda-valanédu, Rajaraja- 
valanidu, Rajéndraginga-valanaidu and Uyyakkondar-valanidu. The first 
member of each of the foregoing names, wz. Pandyakulasani, Nittavindda and Rajén- 
draginga were probably surnames of the king himself. At any rate, we know from the 
large Leyden plates (1. 70 f.) that Nityavinéda wasa surname of Rajaraja.

458 INSCRIPTIONS ‘OF THE RAJARAJESVARA TEMPLE, TANJAVUR. 


No. 95. ‘ON THE OUTSIDE ‘UF THE NORTH ENCLOSURE. ' 


This is not a fresh inseription ‘but only a continuation of No. 64, where the first two 
lines of it are published. A separate number has been given to it here in order to facilitate 
reference. Like Nos. 63 and 94 the subjoined inscription is a list of shepherds who had to 
supply ghee for lamps to the RijardjéSvara temple. The former contain a list of allot 
ments to various shepherds from the cattle presented to the temple by Rajarijadéva 
himself,” while in Nos. 64 and 96, the cattle given by the king, those given by other donors 
and those which were represented by funds as kdsu and akkam deposited in the temple 
treasury for purchase of cattle,’ are assigned ‘to various shepherds under the same conditions 
as have been specified in No. 63. Accordingly, Nos. 64 and 95 have to be treated as a 
continuation of No. 63. 

As in No. 94, the shepherds residing in the vicinity of Tanjore received a lar ge 
number of cows and ewes. The following streets and other localities near Tanjore are 
mentioned in this connection: —-Gandharva-teru, Anaikkaduvar- teru ,Anaiyatkal- 
teru, Panmaiydr-teru, Virasérapperunderu, Rijavi dy4dhyarappornndern, 
Tribhuvanamadévippérangadi, Uyyakkondan -terinda-tirumafijanattar-vé - 
lam, Rajaraja-terinda-Pandi-tirumaijanattar-vélam, UttamaSiliyar-vélam, 
Abhimanabhtfishana-terinda-vélam, Pafichavanmadéviyar-vélam! and Sivad4- 
sané6lai alias Rajaraja-Brahma-maharéjau-padaividu.® Randramahakaélattu- 
madaviligam® (paragraph 5) and Brahmakuttam (paragraph 6) were evidently 
called after the temple situated in eachof them. Of these two, Brahmakuttam is 
already known to usfrom No. 66 above. The foregoing streets, temples and other ‘quarters 
of the town are said to have been outside (purambadi) Taiijavir. Saliyatteru 
(paragraphs 7 and 16) and Pandi-vélam (paragraphs 31 and 48) are the only localities 
mentioned as having been inside (wl/dlaz) Taiijavar. 

In describing the villages of shepherds who did not belong to Tanjore or its neighbour- 
hood, the following territorial divisions are mentioned :—PandyakulaSani-valanadu, 
Rajéndrasimha-valanadu, Rajaraéja-valanddu, Nittavindda-valanidu, Kshat- 
riyaéikhamani-valanadu,’ Kéralantaka-valanidu, Vada- Konadu * and Arumor- 
idéva-valanadu. Kéralantaka-valandidu and Kshatriyasikhimani- 
valanadu were called after the surnames Kéralantakan and Kshatriyasikh4mani, 
probably of the king himself. As regards some of the other names, see p. 437 above. 

Donations in the shape of money, she-buffaloes, cows and ewes are referred to as having 
been made by the king’s officers, and other individuals and groups of men, for burning lamps 
in the temple. Among the donors are a number of people who appear to have contributed

under special circumstances. Military operations were evidently going on, perhaps towards 
the close of RAjaraéja’s reign, at Kéri, 7e. Uraiyur, the ancient Chola capital. A 
number of the king’s officers who were in some way or other connected with the battle were 
probably afraid they would be disgraced by the king for the part they had plaved in it. 
These officers seem to have vowed to put up lamps in the temple, in case they escaped from 
being disgraced. The names of 14 donors for the maintenance of such lamps are mentioned. 
Eight of them have the title perundaram prefixed to their names, viz. (1) Uttaraigu. 
daiyin' Ké6n Vidividangan alas Viliavan- Miivéndavélin; (Z) Mardyan Raja- 
rijan; (3) Kandaradchchan Pattdlaganalias Nittavinédda-Virupparaiyan; (4) 
Alattirudaiyin Kalan Kannappan alas Raijakésari-Miivéndavélin; (5) Loka- 
maraéyan; (6)Rajakésari-Mavéndavélin;* (7) Vayiri Sangaran; and (8) Kévan 
Tayilayyau. The proper name of a ninth person with the same title 1s lost. Three out 
of the fourteen donors were arbitrators (xaduvirukkai seyda) and were apparently Brahmanas, 
viz. (1) Savindi-Pattan Pivattan Pivattanir and (2) Suvara-Pattan Piivatta- 
Pattanar of Kottaiy fir, who were residing at Kamaravalli-chaturvédimangalam, 
a brahmadéya in Mirai-kfirram, a subdivision of Rajéndrasimha-valandidu and 
(3) Daimédara-[Pattan] of Kadalangudi.® One of the donors was the séndpati 
Kuravan Ulagalandaun' alias Rajardja-mahirajan. The last of the fourteen 
donors was Amudan Tévan alias Rajavidydidhara-Virupparaiyan of Sembangudi 
in Avtir-ktrvam, a subdivision of Nittavinéda-valanddu 

The foregoing donations were made apparently in fulfilment of vows. There were 
also others which were evidently made out of a desire to acquire merit. Two of the persons 
mentioned in the preceding paragraph occur also among the voluntary donors, o7z, (1) 
Amudan Tévan alas Rajavidyddhara-Virupparaiyan and (2) the sénépati 
Kuravan Ulagalandan alias Rajaraja-maharajan. Three others, whose names are 
already known to us from other Tanjore,inscriptions, arc also mentioned among the voluntary 
donois, viz (1) Adittan Stryan ahkas Teunavan-Mivéndavélin, the headman of 
Poygai-nddu; (2) frayiravan Pallavayan alias Mummadi-Séra-Pégan of 
Araigtr in Pimbuni-kflrram, a subdivision of Nittavindda-valanidu; and (3) 
the royal secretary Kariyil Eduttapddam, the headman of Rajakésarinallir. 

Among new names, Ilidamadéviy4dr (paragraphs 27, 34 and 56) is the most important. 
She was evidently a queen of Rajaradjadéva. She set up an image of Pigupatamart- 
tigal and presented cows and she-buffaloes for lamps to the image. In all probability, 
her gift of this nnage is recorded in some inscription in the inner enclosure of the temple 
which is still buried underground. ‘The same is probably the case with another inscription 
which must have registered the setting up of the image to whicl. the priest TéanaSiva- 
Pandita presented money for lamps (paragraph 82). Another new name which is interest- 
ing is the Perundaram Kari kala-Karna (or Kanna)-Pallavaraiyan (paramiaphs 64 
and 69) who may be identical with the Perundaram Vayiri Arumori alias Karik4la- 


' This term means that he was a native of Uttarangudi; compare the expression Qerbuaee éOrndud-

Karna-Pallavaraiyan (paragraph 79). He was apparently called after a prince named 
Karikala-Karna. If this be the case, we may suppose that RAjardja’s elder brother 
Aditya-Karikala had a son named Karikala-Karna! (or Krishna) whe was probably 
living during the reign of Rajardija. ; 
The other names of individuals which occur among the voluntary donors are :—(1) the 
merchant (vyépdérin) Achchan Kénarkkaédan alias Rajavidyadhara-mayilatti; (2) 
the Perundaram Rajaraja- Vanakévaraiyan, (perhaps identical with Vanégavaraiyan 
of paragraph 43 and with the Perundaram Vanakévaraiyan of paragraph 53); (3) the 
Sénagan® Savar Parafjodi; (4) Padi Sattan, the headman of Midfr; (5) the 
Perundaram Namban K ittadi alias Jayangonda-Sé6ra- Brahma-mahar4jan ; (6) the 
Perundaram Tirumalai Vengadan, the headman of Vayaltr; (7) the Perundaram Koén 
Stirri alias Arumori-Pallavaraiyan (perhaps identical with the Perundaram Arumori- 
Pallavaraiyan of paragraph 70) and (8) the Perundaram Nittavinéda-maharajan. 
The following groups of men are also mentioned among the donors :— 
(1) Udaiy4r-Sri-Rajarajadévar-Mummadi-Séra-terinda-parivarattar, 
(2) Jananatha-terinda-parivarattar, 
(3) Malaparivara-vittéru adas Janandtha-terinda-parivarattar and 
(1) Palavagai-param-padaigalilar.

? At the end of this line, the engraver seems to liave taken the precaution to leave some space between 
two successive letters, perhaps in order that he may fill the line with the words found in it on the original 
from which he copied. This spicing begins from tke word pnreng7B.

1 Another possible’ translation of the passage sar ter scoLwre Ufmreartea@2at OerPiQurdr 
astsos slid cern a.a SQGdSerée is- “sacred lamps, (which he) had (vowed) to put up (an ease) 
the lord Sri-Rajarajadéva did not throw filth (ov) him in the warat Koi.’ Dr Iultssch tentatively trans- 
lated the passage by ‘‘a sacred lamp, which he had vowed (to put up) because the lord Sri-Rajarajadéva did 
not take his lifein the battle of Kori,” and ina footnote remarked : ‘‘the donor appears to have incurred 
Rajaraja’s disfavour for having lost the battle, but to have been subsequently pardoned.” There are 14 
people mentioned in this inscription who presented lamps under identical circumstances. Their names will 
appear in the following paragraphs. They appear to have been officers of the king. and one of them was a 
general, while three were Biahmanas, There seems to be no doubt omane point, (viz.) that the circumstances 
which led the donors to provide for the lamps are not fully explained in the words ttfat aftém J, Unless 

we get the full text of the w oid. in some other inscription, no certainty can be arrived at An, xegards the 
interpretation of the words in question. K6riis another name of Uiaiytr, which was once the C dle capital, 
It has, therefore, to be presumed it was a riot or a rebellion at Kéri that had to be suppressed. ‘Taking all the 
circumstances into account 1t looks as if these officers were connected in some w ay or other with the revolt ut 
Kéri. It may be that these ofhcers vowed to put up lamps in the temple im case they did not incur the 
tlisfavour of the king. See also my remarks on page 458 f. above.

No. 96. In THE SECOND GUPURA OF THE ‘TEMPLE, RIGHT OF EN'TRANCE, ! 


The subjoined inscription is dated in the 3rd year of the reign of Tribhuvana- 
chakravartin Rajaréjadéva, ze, Rajaraja III. It does not record any gift, but 
registers a political compact entered into by three chiefs of the Ch 61a country to be faithful 
to the king and to stand by each other. The compact shows that the Ch6é1la country was 
disturbed, perhaps by internal dissensions and that the Chéla capital Tanjore was not 
frec from them. As this is dated in the 3rd year of RA jaraja III., it may be presumed that 
the Chéla country became disturbed already towards the end of the reign of Kuléttunga 
III. Two such compacts belonging to about the same period are registered at Sen gama 
in the South Arcot District. ° 

By Ulagudaiya-Nayanadr Sripaddam (1.13 f, and 17f.) Rajaraja Ill, is 
probably meant.

Wail! Prosperity! In the 3rd year' (of the reign) of the emperor of the three worlds,

No. 97.-——Is THE srcoND GoPURA OF THE TEMPLE, RIGHT OF ENTRANOE.' 


The subjoined inscription records that the goldsmiths of Tanjore were exempted from 
taxes by the Nayaka chief Achyutappa- Nayaka, son of Sevvappa-Niyaka 
of Tanjore. It is dated in Saka-Sathvat 1499 expired, the cyclic year Bahudhinya

TRANSLATION. 

Let there be prosperity ! Hail! Prosperity ! 

On the auspicious occasion of the prathamadvddasi’, a Monday (corresponding to) the 
1[8]th solar day of the month of Ani in the year Bahudhanya, which was current after (the. 
expiry of) the Saka year 1499—(the following ts) the charter (conveying) a lawful edict given 
by Achyutappa-Nayakkar Ayyan, (son of) Sevvappa-N ayakkar Ayyan to 
the goldsmiths of Taiijdviir. At the request of Kandanélu Naraya Kurunidan 
(and) SiagappalliNagabatta Séshastiri (ac. perhaps SéshAdri), your taxes (‘rat) 
have been caused to be remitted, and (thze) shall continue as long as the moon and the sun 
(endure) as a remission of all taxes (sarvamdnya). One who obstructs this charity shall incur 
the sin of having killed tawny cows on the bank of the Genges. One who obstructs this 
charity shall incur the sin of having pulled out a thousand Jitigas.
